Discrete equations from Bäcklund transformations of the fifth Painlevé equation
Peter A. Clarkson, Clare Dunning, Ben Mitchell, [5pt] School of Engineering, Mathematics, PhysicsUniversity of Kent, Canterbury, CT2 7NF, UK, [5pt] School of Mathematics, University of Leeds, Leeds, LS2 9JT, UK[5pt] Email: P.A.Clarkson@kent.ac.uk, T.C.Dunning@leeds.ac.uk, bsjm2@kent.ac.uk

Abstract
In this paper discrete equations are derived from Bäcklund transformations of the fifth Painlevé equation, including a new discrete equation which has ternary symmetry. There are two classes of rational solutions of the fifth Painlevé equation, one expressed in terms of the generalised Laguerre polynomials and the other in terms of the generalised Umemura polynomials, both of which can be expressed as Wronskians of Laguerre polynomials. Hierarchies of rational solutions of the discrete equations are derived in terms of the generalised Laguerre and generalised Umemura polynomials. It is known that there is non-uniqueness of some rational solutions of the fifth Painlevé equation. Pairs of non-unique rational solutions are used to derive distinct hierarchies of rational solutions which satisfy the same discrete equation.



Mathematics Subject Classification (2020)
: 33E17 34M55 37J70 39A10

1
Introduction

In this paper we are concerned with discrete Painlevé equations associated with the fifth Painlevé equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\left(\frac{1}{2w}+\frac{1}{w-1}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{(w-1)^{2}(\alpha w^{2}+{\beta})}{z^{2}w}+\frac{\gamma w}{z}+\frac{\delta w(w+1)}{w-1},

(1.1)

with
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\delta
constants. We consider the generic case of (
1.1
) when
\delta\not=0
, so we set

\delta=-\tfrac{1}{2}
, without loss of generality (by rescaling
z
if necessary) and obtain

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\left(\frac{1}{2w}+\frac{1}{w-1}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{(w-1)^{2}(\alpha w^{2}+{\beta})}{z^{2}w}+\frac{\gamma w}{z}-\frac{w(w+1)}{2(w-1)},

(1.2)

which we will refer to as
P
V
. In the case when
\delta=0
in (
1.1
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\left(\frac{1}{2w}+\frac{1}{w-1}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{(w-1)^{2}(\alpha w^{2}+{\beta})}{z^{2}w}+\frac{\gamma w}{z},

(1.3)

which is known as the
degenerate fifth Painlevé equation
(deg-
P
V
).

The six Painlevé equations were discovered by Painlevé, Gambier and their colleagues in the late 19th and early 20th centuries whilst studying second order ordinary differential equations of the form

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=F\left(z,w,\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right),

(1.4)

where
F
is rational in
\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
and
w
and analytic in
z
. The Painlevé transcendents, i.e. the solutions of the Painlevé equations, can be thought of as nonlinear analogues of the classical special functions.
The Painlevé equations are regarded as being “completely integrable” and solvable using Riemann-Hilbert techniques
[
25
]
.
The general solutions of the Painlevé equations are transcendental in the sense that they cannot be expressed in terms of known elementary functions and so require the introduction of a new transcendental function to describe their solution. However, it is well known that all the Painlevé equations, except
P
I
, possess rational solutions, algebraic solutions and solutions expressed in terms of the classical special functions for special values of the parameters, see, e.g.
[
14
,
38
]

and the references therein. These hierarchies are usually generated from “seed solutions” using the associated Bäcklund transformations and frequently can be expressed in the form of determinants.

Discrete Painlevé equations also have a long history. The discrete Painlevé I (d
P
I
) equation is

x_{n+1}+x_{n}+x_{n-1}=1+\frac{\lambda n+\rho}{x_{n}},

(1.5)

with
\lambda
and
\rho
constants. Equation (
1.5
) was studied in 1939 by Shohat
[
75
]
, developing ideas due to Laguerre
[
47
]
dating from 1885, in the study of orthogonal polynomials associated with the quartic weight
\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}x^{4})
on the real line.
Subsequently this problem was studied by Freud
[
28
]
, see also
[
8
]
, though it was not recognised at the time as a discrete Painlevé equation.
Equation (
1.5
) was then rediscovered in the 1980s in models of gauge ﬁeld theory by Bessis, Itzykson and Zuber
[
7
]
and in quantum gravity by Brézin and Kazakov
[
9
]
, who showed that its continuum limit is the first Painlevé equation (
P
I
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=6w^{2}+z,

(1.6)

see also
[
24
]
.

The discrete Painlevé II (d
P
II
) equation is

x_{n+1}+x_{n-1}=\frac{\zeta[(n+\lambda)x_{n}+\rho]}{1-x_{n}^{2}},

(1.7)

with
\zeta
,
\lambda
and
\rho
constants, which was derived by Periwal and Shevitz
[
68
]
in a study of unitary matrix models in a double scaling limit using orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle.
Around the same time, Nijhoff and Papageorgiou
[
55
]
independently derived d
P
II
(
1.7
) and showed that its continuum limit is the second Painlevé equation (
P
II
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=2w^{3}+zw+\alpha,

(1.8)

with
\alpha
a constant; Periwal and Shevitz
[
68
]
gave a continuum limit to
P
II
(
1.8
) with
\alpha=0
.

One method for deriving discrete Painlevé equations is using Bäcklund transformations of (continuous) Painlevé equations. For example, it is well known that
P
II
(
1.8
) has the Bäcklund transformations

\displaystyle w(z;\alpha+1)

\displaystyle=-w(z;\alpha)-\frac{2\alpha+1}{2w^{2}(z;\alpha)+2w^{\prime}(z;\alpha)+z},

(1.9a)

\displaystyle w(z;\alpha-1)

\displaystyle=-w(z;\alpha)-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2w^{2}(z;\alpha)-2w^{\prime}(z;\alpha)+z}.

(1.9b)

with
{}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

[
22
,
30
]
.
Hence letting
x_{n}=w(z;n+\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2})
, with
\alpha=n+\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}
, then by eliminating
w^{\prime}(z;\alpha)
we obtain another discrete equation, commonly known as alt-d
P
I

\frac{n+\lambda}{x_{n+1}+x_{n}}+\frac{n+\lambda-1}{x_{n}+x_{n-1}}+2x_{n}^{2}+z=0,

(1.10)

which has
P
I
(
1.6
) as its continuum limit
[
23
]
.
The alt-d
P
I
equation (
1.10
) appeared in 1981 in the work of Jimbo and Miwa
[
41
, equation (5.3)]
, though it was not recognised to be a discrete Painlevé equation until the work of Fokas, Grammaticos and Ramani
[
23
]
.

In some respects the discrete Painlevé equations are much richer than the Painlevé equations. For example, they can have solutions which have no continuum limit and other properties which are lost in the continuous limit. Perhaps the most fundamental difference is that, whilst the Painlevé equations are unique up to a Möbius
transformation, this is not true for the discrete Painlevé equations. For each discrete Painlevé equation there exists a number of different forms, as illustrated by d
P
I
(
1.5
) and alt-d
P
I
(
1.10
) which both have
P
I
(
1.6
) as a continuum limit yet are not equivalent as there is no transformation relating the solutions (
1.5
) and (
1.10
).
Further their exact solutions are quite different since d
P
I
(
1.5
) arises from Bäcklund transformations of the fourth Painlevé equation (
P
IV
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2w}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}+\tfrac{3}{2}w^{3}+4zw^{2}+2(z^{2}-\alpha)w+\frac{\beta}{w},

(1.11)

with
\alpha
and
\beta
constants
[
32
]
, whereas alt-d
P
I
(
1.10
) arises from Bäcklund transformations of
P
II
(
1.8
), as shown above.

The nomenclature for discrete Painlevé equations can be misleading. The original naming of discrete Painlevé equations came from their continuum limits but this can be ambiguous. For example, there are generalisations of d
P
I
(
1.5
) and d
P
II
(
1.7
), see equations (
3.32
) and (
3.7
), respectively, which have continuum limits to other Painlevé equations, as discussed below. Another approach is to classify the discrete Painlevé equations by the type of affine Weyl group and associated affine Dynkin diagram they are derived from, which is now commonly used.
Sakai
[
73
]
, extending the earlier work of Okamoto
[
63
]
for the (continuous) Painlevé equations, approached the discrete Painlevé equations from the point of view of algebraic geometry. Sakai clarified the algebraic nature of discrete Painlevé equations and gave a classification scheme. A comprehensive review of the geometric aspects of the continuous and discrete Painlevé equations is given by Kajiwara, Noumi and Yamada
[
44
]
. There is a recent discussion of the symmetry group of d
P
II
(
1.7
) in
[
20
]
. Discrete Painlevé equations arise in various applications such as random matrix theory and orthogonal polynomials, cf.
[
80
,
81
]
.

There are three distinct types of discrete Painlevé equations: (i), those for which the discrete variable is additive, which is the case for the discrete equations we discuss; (ii), the
q
-discrete Painlevé equations for which the discrete variable is multiplicative; and (iii), elliptic discrete Painlevé equations whose coefficients are elliptic functions of the independent variable
n
.

From the works of Okamoto

[
64
,
65
,
66
,
67
]
, it is known that the parameter spaces of
P
II
–
P
VI
all admit the action of an
extended affine Weyl group; the group acts as a group of Bäcklund transformations.
In a series of papers, Noumi and Yamada
[
56
,
57
,
59
,
61
]

have implemented this idea to derive a hierarchy of dynamical systems
associated to the affine Weyl group of type
\widetilde{A}_{N}^{(1)}
, often known as “
symmetric forms of the Painlevé equations
”. The behaviour of each dynamical
system varies depending on whether
N
is even or odd.
The first member of the
\widetilde{A}_{2n+1}^{(1)}
hierarchy, i.e.
\widetilde{A}_{3}^{(1)}
, usually known as symmetric-
P
V
(s
P
V
), is equivalent to
P
V
(
1.2
)
and given by

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{f_{0}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=f_{0}f_{2}(f_{1}-f_{3})+(\tfrac{1}{2}-\vartheta_{2})f_{0}+\vartheta_{0}f_{2},

(1.12a)

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{f_{1}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=f_{1}f_{3}(f_{2}-f_{0})+(\tfrac{1}{2}-\vartheta_{3})f_{1}+\vartheta_{1}f_{3},

(1.12b)

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{f_{2}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=f_{2}f_{0}(f_{3}-f_{1})+(\tfrac{1}{2}-\vartheta_{0})f_{2}+\vartheta_{2}f_{0},

(1.12c)

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{f_{3}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=f_{3}f_{1}(f_{0}-f_{2})+(\tfrac{1}{2}-\vartheta_{1})f_{3}+\vartheta_{3}f_{1},

(1.12d)

with the normalisations

f_{0}(z)+f_{2}(z)=\sqrt{z},\qquad f_{1}(z)+f_{3}(z)=\sqrt{z},

(1.12e)

and
\vartheta_{0}
,
\vartheta_{1}
,
\vartheta_{2}
and
\vartheta_{3}
are constants such that

\vartheta_{0}+\vartheta_{1}+\vartheta_{2}+\vartheta_{3}=1.

(1.12f)

The symmetric system s
P
V
(
1.12
) was found by Adler
[
1
]
in the context of periodic chains of Bäcklund transformations and has applications in random matrix theory, cf.
[
27
]
.

The outline of this paper is as follows.
In §
2
we describe Bäcklund transformations of
P
V
(
1.2
).
In §
3
we derive some discrete Painlevé equations from these Bäcklund transformations, including a new discrete equation which has a ternary symmetry. There have been earlier studies deriving discrete Painlevé equations from Bäcklund transformations of
P
V
(
1.2
), see
[
23
,
78
]
. Our study explicitly gives the Bäcklund transformations which generate the discrete Painlevé equations. These then can be used to obtain exact solutions of the discrete equations in terms of known solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
).
In §
4
we describe the rational solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
) which are expressed in terms of Wronskians of Laguerre polynomials.
In §
5
we derive hierarchies of rational solutions of the discrete Painlevé equations derived in §
3
in terms of the rational solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
) given in §
4
. Further pairs of rational solutions which although different, which satisfy
P
V
(
1.2
) for the same parameters, are used to derive distinct hierarchies of rational solutions that satisfy the same discrete equation. Finally in §
6
we discuss our results.

2
Bäcklund transformations of the fifth Painlevé equation

In this section we describe the Bäcklund transformations of
P
V
(
1.2
).

Definition 2.1
.

A
Bäcklund transformation
maps a solution of a given Painlevé equation to another solution of the same Painlevé equation,
with different values of the parameters, or another equation.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
w=w(z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
be a solution of
P
V
(
1.2
) such that

\Phi(w;z)=z\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\varepsilon_{2}aw^{2}+(\varepsilon_{2}a-\varepsilon_{3}b+\varepsilon_{1}z)w+\varepsilon_{3}b\not=0,

(2.1)

and

\Phi(w;z)-2\varepsilon_{1}zw\not=0,

(2.2)

where
a=\sqrt{2\alpha}
and
b=\sqrt{-2\beta}
and
\gamma=c
. Then the
Bäcklund transformation

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}
is defined by

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}:\quad W(z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1-\frac{2\varepsilon_{1}zw}{\Phi(w;z)}=1-\frac{2\varepsilon_{1}zw}{zw^{\prime}-\varepsilon_{2}aw^{2}+(\varepsilon_{2}a-\varepsilon_{3}b+\varepsilon_{1}z)w+\varepsilon_{3}b},

(2.3)

with
{}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
,
\varepsilon_{j}=\pm 1
,
j=1,2,3
, independently, and for the parameters

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}(a,b,c)=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big[c+\varepsilon_{1}(1-\varepsilon_{3}b-\varepsilon_{2}a)\big],\tfrac{1}{2}\big(c-\varepsilon_{1}(1-\varepsilon_{3}b-\varepsilon_{2}a)\big),\varepsilon_{1}(\varepsilon_{3}b-\varepsilon_{2}a)\Big),

(2.4)

cf.
[
34
,
35
,
36
]
,
[
38
, §39]
or
[
62
, §32.7(v)]
.

Note that we are using the same notation as in
[
62
, §32.7(v)]
which slightly differs from that used in
[
34
,
35
,
36
]
or

[
38
, §39]
.

Remarks 2.3
.

1.

Since
\varepsilon_{j}=\pm 1
,
j=1,2,3
, independently, there are eight distinct transformations
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}
. The parameter
\varepsilon_{1}
reflects that the transformation
(z,\gamma)\to(-z,-\gamma)
is a discrete symmetry of
P
V
. The parameters
\varepsilon_{2}
and
\varepsilon_{3}
reflect that
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{2}
and
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{2}b^{2}
, respectively, so
a
and
b
can take either sign.

2.

The Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}
is usually given for the parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, in terms of
a
,
b
and
c
. However in order to apply a sequence of Bäcklund transformations, it’s preferable to define the action of Bäcklund transformation on the parameters
a
,
b
and
c
, rather than
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, to avoid any ambiguity in taking a square root.

3.

We note that the Hamiltonian for
P
V
(
1.2
) in
[
41
]
is written in terms of the monodromy parameters
\theta_{0}
,
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{\infty}
, which are related to the parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
through

\alpha=\tfrac{1}{8}(\theta_{0}-\theta_{1}+\theta_{\infty})^{2},\qquad\beta=-\tfrac{1}{8}(\theta_{0}-\theta_{1}-\theta_{\infty})^{2},\qquad\gamma=1-\theta_{0}-\theta_{1},

(2.5)

see also
[
65
]
, as are the Schlesinger transformations, which relate two solutions of the associated monodromy problem, for
P
V
(
1.2
), see
[
26
,
54
]
.
Further the discrete symmetries of
P
V
(
1.2
), corresponding to the extended affine Weyl group of type
A_{3}^{(1)}
, act naturally on the root variables, see, for example,
[
17
]
. Hence it is natural to define the Bäcklund transformations for
P
V
(
1.2
) in terms of the parameters
a=\sqrt{2\alpha}
,
b=\sqrt{-2\beta}
and
c=\gamma
, rather than
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
.

4.

When applying the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}
followed by the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}
we use the notation
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}
, i.e.

\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}(w;a,b,c)\big)=\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}(w;a,b,c).

3
Deriving discrete Painlevé equations

In this section we derive four discrete Painlevé equations which are associated with
P
V
(
1.2
) using the Bäcklund transformations described in §
2
.

To derive a discrete Painlevé equation from Bäcklund transformations of
P
V
(
1.2
), we use the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{+}
, which relates a solution
w(z;a,b,c)
to another solution
w_{+}(z;a_{+},b_{+},c_{+})
and its inverse transformation
\mathcal{R}_{-}=\mathcal{R}_{+}^{-1}
, which relates
w(z;a,b,c)
to a third solution
w_{-}(z;a_{-},b_{-},c_{-})
. Eliminating the derivative between the two Bäcklund transformations gives an algebraic relation between
w(z;a,b,c)
,
w_{+}(z;a_{+},b_{+},c_{+})
and
w_{-}(z;a_{-},b_{-},c_{-})
, which is the discrete equation.
Then setting
w(z;a,b,c)=w_{n}
,
{w_{\pm}}(z;a_{\pm},b_{\pm},c_{\pm})=w_{n\pm 1}
, with

(a,b,c)=(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})
and
(a_{\pm},b_{\pm},c_{\pm})=(a_{n\pm 1},b_{n\pm 1},c_{n\pm 1})
, gives the chain

\{w_{n-1},{a}_{n-1},{b}_{n-1},{c}_{n-1}\}\enskip\begin{array}[]{c}{\scriptstyle{\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}_{+}}}}\\[-8.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow\\[-8.0pt]
\longleftarrow\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\\[-7.0pt]
{\scriptstyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{-}}}\end{array}\enskip\{w_{n},a_{n},b_{n},c_{n}\}\enskip\begin{array}[]{c}{\scriptstyle{\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}_{+}}}}\\[-8.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!\longrightarrow\\[-8.0pt]
\longleftarrow\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\!\!\!-\\[-7.0pt]
{\scriptstyle{\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}_{-}}}}\end{array}\enskip\{w_{n+1},{a}_{n+1},{b}_{n+1},{c}_{n+1}\}.

The parameters satisfy a linear discrete system derived from (
2.4
).
See, for example,
[
18
,
23
]
for more details of this procedure.

Remarks 3.1
.

1.

It is essential that
\mathcal{R}_{-}
is the inverse transformation of
\mathcal{R}_{+}
. It is possible to create an algebraic relation between three solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
) using two Bäcklund transformations
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
when
\mathcal{R}_{2}\not=\mathcal{R}_{1}^{-1}
, but this won’t necessarily result in a discrete Painlevé equation. For example, in
[
38
, §39]
, see also
[
35
,
36
]
, there are algebraic relations of three (and four) solutions of
P
V
, but these are not discrete Painlevé equations.

2.

The Bessel function
J_{n}(z)
satisfies the differential equation

z^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{J_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}+z\frac{\mathrm{d}{J_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+(z^{2}-n^{2})J_{n}=0,

(3.1)

and the recurrence relations

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{J_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}(z)=zJ_{n-1}(z)-nJ_{n}(z),

(3.2a)

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{J_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}(z)=nJ_{n}(z)-zJ_{n+1}(z),

(3.2b)

\displaystyle zJ_{n+1}(z)-2nJ_{n}(z)+zJ_{n-1}(z)=0,

(3.2c)

cf.
[
62
, §10]
. Eliminating
\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{J_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}(z)
between (
3.2a
) and
(
3.2b
) yields the recurrence relation (
3.2c
). Letting
n\to n+1
in (
3.2a
) and eliminating
J_{n+1}(z)
using
(
3.2b
) gives the differential equation (
3.1
).
Thus the Bessel function
J_{n}(z)
satisfies both the differential equation (
3.1
) and the difference equation (
3.2c
). An analogous situation occurs for the Painlevé equations, except
P
I
(
1.6
).

In the next three subsections, we derive discrete equations using the Bäcklund transformations

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1},&\mathcal{R}_{1}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1},\\
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1},&\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{1,1,1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1},\\
\mathcal{R}_{3}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1},&\mathcal{R}_{3}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1},\end{array}

as well as a discrete equation which has a ternary symmetry using the Bäcklund transformation

\mathcal{R}_{4}=\mathcal{R}_{3}^{2}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}^{2}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{4}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1}^{2}.

3.1
First discrete equation: asymmetric discrete
P
II

Consider the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}
which has an inverse
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}
, then

\displaystyle w_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{1}(w_{n})=1-\frac{2zw_{n}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}+a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(a_{n}+b_{n}-z)w_{n}+b_{n}},

(3.3a)

\displaystyle w_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{1}^{-1}(w_{n})=1+\frac{2zw_{n}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}-a_{n}w_{n}^{2}+(a_{n}+b_{n}-z)w_{n}-b_{n}},

(3.3b)

with
{}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
,
where
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
satisfy

\displaystyle(a_{n+1},b_{n+1},c_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{1}(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}-b_{n}+c_{n}+1\big),\tfrac{1}{2}\big(-a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}-1\big),a_{n}+b_{n}\right),

\displaystyle(a_{n-1},b_{n-1},c_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{1}^{-1}(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}-b_{n}+c_{n}-1\big),\tfrac{1}{2}\big(-a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}+1\big),a_{n}+b_{n}\right),

which have solution

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\big[n+\lambda+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big],\qquad b_{n}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big[n+\lambda-\rho-(-1)^{n}\varphi\big],\qquad c_{n}=\rho-(-1)^{n}\varphi,

(3.4)

with
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\varphi
arbitrary constants.
From (
3.3
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w_{n+1}-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2w_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(a_{n}+b_{n}-z)w_{n}+b_{n}}{2zw_{n}}=0,

(3.5a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w_{n-1}-1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2w_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(a_{n}+b_{n}-z)w_{n}+b_{n}}{2zw_{n}}=0,

(3.5b)

so adding gives

\frac{1}{w_{n+1}-1}+\frac{1}{w_{n-1}-1}+\frac{a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(a_{n}+b_{n}-z)w_{n}+b_{n}}{zw_{n}}=0,

which is equation (3.4) in
[
37
]
.

Setting
w_{n}=(q_{n}+1)/(q_{n}-1)
and
w_{n\pm 1}=(q_{n\pm 1}+1)/(q_{n\pm 1}-1)
in (
3.3
) gives

\displaystyle q_{n+1}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{1-q_{n}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{2}{z}\,\frac{(n+\lambda)q_{n}+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi}{1-q_{n}^{2}}=0,

(3.6a)

\displaystyle q_{n-1}

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{1-q_{n}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{2}{z}\,\frac{(n+\lambda)q_{n}+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi}{1-q_{n}^{2}}=0.

(3.6b)

Hence by adding we obtain

q_{n+1}+q_{n-1}=\frac{4}{z}\frac{(n+\lambda)q_{n}+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi}{1-q_{n}^{2}},

(3.7)

which is known as the
asymmetric
d
P
II
equation

[
74
]
and is a generalisation of d
P
II
(
1.7
).
Further
q_{n}
satisfies the second-order equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{q_{n}}{q_{n}^{2}-1}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{a_{n}^{2}(q_{n}+1)^{2}-b_{n}^{2}(q_{n}-1)^{2}}{z^{2}(q_{n}^{2}-1)}-\frac{c_{n}(q_{n}^{2}-1)}{2z}+\tfrac{1}{4}q_{n}(q_{n}^{2}-1),

(3.8)

with
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
given by (
3.4
).

In order to consider the alternating term in (
3.7
), i.e. the binary symmetry, we set
q_{2n}=x_{n}
and
q_{2n+1}=y_{n}
in (
3.7
) and obtain the discrete system

\displaystyle x_{n+1}+x_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{z}\frac{(2n+\lambda+1)y_{n}+\rho-\varphi}{1-y_{n}^{2}},\qquad y_{n}+y_{n-1}=\frac{4}{z}\frac{(2n+\lambda)x_{n}+\rho+\varphi}{1-x_{n}^{2}},

(3.9)

which is regarded as a discrete
P
III
equation (d
P
III
) since it admits a continuous limit to the third Painlevé equation (
P
III
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{1}{w}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{\alpha w^{2}+\beta}{w}+\gamma w^{3}+\frac{\delta}{w},

(3.10)

with
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\delta
constants
[
31
,
72
]
.

We note that

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2}(a,b,c)=(a+1,b-1,c),

and so

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2n}(a,b,c)=(n+a,b-n,c).

Therefore since

\displaystyle(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+\rho+\varphi),-\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda-\rho-\varphi),\rho-\varphi\big),

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+\rho-\varphi+1),-\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda-\rho+\varphi+1),\rho+\varphi\big),

then

\displaystyle(a_{2n},b_{2n},c_{2n})

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(2n+\lambda+\rho+\varphi\big),-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(2n+\lambda-\rho-\varphi\big),\rho-\varphi\Big),

\displaystyle(a_{2n+1},b_{2n+1},c_{2n+1})

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(2n+1+\lambda+\rho-\varphi\big),-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(2n+1+\lambda-\rho+\varphi\big),\rho+\varphi\Big).

If
w_{0}(z)
satisfies
P
V
(
1.2
) with
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{8}(\lambda+\rho+\varphi)^{2}
,
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{8}(\lambda-\rho-\varphi)^{2}
and
\gamma=\rho-\varphi
then

x_{n}=q_{2n}=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2n}(w_{0})+1}{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2n}(w_{0})-1},\qquad y_{n}=q_{2n+1}=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2n+1}(w_{0})+1}{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2n+1}(w_{0})-1},

(3.11)

satisfy (
3.9
).

Remarks 3.2
.

1.

In the case when
\rho=\varphi=0
, equation (
3.8
) becomes

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{q_{n}}{q_{n}^{2}-1}\left\{\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{(4n+\lambda)^{2}}{z^{2}}\right\}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\tfrac{1}{4}q_{n}(q_{n}^{2}-1),

(3.12)

which is equivalent to
P
V
(
1.2
) with
\alpha=-\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\lambda)^{2}
and
\gamma=0
.
Making the transformation
q_{n}=1/\sqrt{1-u_{n}}
with
z=2\sqrt{x}
in equation (
3.12
) gives deg-
P
V
(
1.3
) with
\alpha=0
,
\beta=-2(n+\lambda)^{2}
and
\gamma=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Solutions of deg-
P
V
(
1.3
) are related to those of
P
III
(
3.10
), cf.
[
38
, Theorem 34.2]
.
Solutions of equation (
3.12
), in terms of Bessel functions, are discussed in
[
15
,
39
]
.

2.

Equation (
3.12
) arises as a reduction of the two-dimensional complex sine-Gordon equation, also known as the Pohlmeyer-Lund-Regge model
[
48
,
49
,
69
]
,
in quantum systems
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
, in relativity
[
29
]
and in coefficients of the three-term recurrence relation for orthogonal polynomials with respect to the weight
w(\theta)=\exp({t\cos\theta})
on the unit circle, see
[
80
, equation (3.13)]
. The orthogonal polynomials for this weight are related to unitary random matrices
[
68
]
.

3.2
Second discrete equation

Consider the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}
which has the inverse
\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{1,1,1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}
, then

\displaystyle w_{n+1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}(w_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}}\left\{1+\frac{(a_{n}-b_{n}-c_{n}-1)(w_{n}-1)^{2}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}-a_{n}w_{n}^{2}+(2a_{n}-c_{n}-1+z)w_{n}-(a_{n}-c_{n}-1)}\right\},

(3.13a)

\displaystyle w_{n-1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}(w_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}}\left\{1-\frac{(a_{n}-b_{n}-c_{n}+1)(w_{n}-1)^{2}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}+a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(2a_{n}-c_{n}+1+z)w_{n}+(a_{n}-c_{n}+1)}\right\}.

(3.13b)

with
{}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
,
where
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
satisfy

\displaystyle(a_{n+1},b_{n+1},c_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{2}(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}+b_{n}-c_{n}-1\big),\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}+1\big),-a_{n}+b_{n}+1\Big),

\displaystyle(a_{n-1},b_{n-1},c_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}+b_{n}-c_{n}+1\big),\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}-1\big),-a_{n}+b_{n}-1\Big),

which have solution

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big[n+\lambda+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big],\qquad b_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2}\big[n+\lambda-\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big],\qquad c_{n}=n+\lambda-(-1)^{n}\varphi,

(3.14)

with
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\varphi
arbitrary constants.
From (
3.13
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{a_{n}-b_{n}-c_{n}-1}{w_{n}w_{n+1}-1}

\displaystyle-\frac{z}{(w_{n}-1)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+a_{n}-\frac{z-c_{n}-1}{w_{n}-1}-\frac{z}{\left(w_{n}-1\right)^{2}}=0,

(3.15a)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{n}-b_{n}-c_{n}+1}{w_{n}w_{n-1}-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{z}{(w_{n}-1)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{w_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+a_{n}-\frac{z-c_{n}+1}{w_{n}-1}-\frac{z}{\left(w_{n}-1\right)^{2}}=0,

(3.15b)

Hence adding
and using (
3.14
) gives the difference equation

\frac{2n+2\lambda+1}{w_{n}w_{n+1}-1}+\frac{2n+2\lambda-1}{w_{n}w_{n-1}-1}+n+\lambda+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi-\frac{2\big[n+\lambda-(-1)^{n}\varphi\big]}{w_{n}-1}+\frac{2zw_{n}}{(w_{n}-1)^{2}}=0,

(3.16)

which is equivalent to equation (2.8) in
[
71
]
.

Setting
w_{n}=(q_{n}+1)/(q_{n}-1)
and
w_{n\pm 1}=(q_{n\pm 1}+1)/(q_{n\pm 1}-1)
in (
3.13
)
gives

\displaystyle\frac{2n+2\lambda+1}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}}

\displaystyle-\frac{z}{1-q_{n}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\tfrac{1}{2}z+\frac{\big[n+\lambda+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big]q_{n}+\rho}{1-q_{n}^{2}}=0,

(3.17a)

\displaystyle\frac{2n+2\lambda-1}{q_{n}+q_{n-1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{z}{1-q_{n}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\tfrac{1}{2}z+\frac{\big[n+\lambda+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big]q_{n}+\rho}{1-q_{n}^{2}}=0.

(3.17b)

Hence by adding we obtain

\frac{2n+2\lambda+1}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}}+\frac{2n+2\lambda-1}{q_{n}+q_{n-1}}=z-\frac{2\big[n+\lambda+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big]q_{n}+2\rho}{1-q_{n}^{2}},

(3.18)

and then letting
\xi_{n}=n+\lambda
gives

\frac{\xi_{n+1}+\xi_{n}}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}}+\frac{\xi_{n}+\xi_{n-1}}{q_{n}+q_{n-1}}=z-\frac{2\big[\xi_{n}+(-1)^{n}\varphi\big]q_{n}+2\rho}{1-q_{n}^{2}}.

(3.19)

This is equation (2.9) in
[
71
]
, equation (3.25) in
[
78
]
and equation (13) in
[
33
]
.
Following
[
71
]
, we introduce a new variable
p_{n}
through

p_{n}=1-\frac{2}{z}\,\frac{\xi_{n+1}+\xi_{n}}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}},

and then (
3.19
) becomes

\displaystyle p_{n}+p_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{z}\,\frac{[\xi_{n}+(-1)^{n}\varphi]q_{n}+\rho}{1-q_{n}^{2}},\qquad q_{n+1}+q_{n}=\frac{2}{z}\,\frac{\xi_{n+1}+\xi_{n}}{1-p_{n}},

(3.20)

which is system (2.10) in
[
71
]
. Ramani, Ohta and Grammaticos
[
71
]
state that “the system (
3.20
) describes a motion in the same parameter space as asymmetric d
P
II
which is associated with the
A_{3}
afﬁne Weyl group. While asymmetric d
P
II
describes a motion along the axes, the geometry of (
3.20
) is more complicated: the associated motion is a ‘staircase’ one”.
To our knowledge
1
1

1

This was confirmed in an email communication with Basil Grammaticos.
, there is no discussion in the literature as to which (continuous) Painlevé equation arises in the continuum limit for either (
3.19
) or (
3.20
).

In order to consider the alternating term in (
3.18
), i.e. the binary symmetry,
setting
q_{2n}=x_{n}
and
q_{2n+1}=y_{n}
yields the discrete system

\displaystyle\frac{4n+2\lambda+3}{x_{n+1}+y_{n}}+\frac{4n+2\lambda+1}{x_{n}+y_{n}}

\displaystyle=z-\frac{2(2n+\lambda-\varphi+1)y_{n}+2\rho}{1-y_{n}^{2}},

(3.21a)

\displaystyle\frac{4n+2\lambda+1}{x_{n}+y_{n}}+\frac{4n+2\lambda-1}{x_{n}+y_{n-1}}

\displaystyle=z-\frac{2(2n+\lambda+\varphi)x_{n}+2\rho}{1-x_{n}^{2}},

(3.21b)

and so if
\xi_{n}=n+\lambda
then

\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{2n+2}+\xi_{2n+1}}{x_{n+1}+y_{n}}+\frac{\xi_{2n+1}+\xi_{2n}}{x_{n}+y_{n}}

\displaystyle=z-\frac{2(\xi_{2n+1}-\varphi)y_{n}+2\rho}{1-y_{n}^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{2n+1}+\xi_{2n}}{x_{n}+y_{n}}+\frac{\xi_{2n}+\xi_{2n-1}}{x_{n}+y_{n-1}}

\displaystyle=z-\frac{2(\xi_{2n}+\varphi)x_{n}+2\rho}{1-x_{n}^{2}}.

Further
q_{n}
satisfies the second-order equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{q_{n}}{q_{n}^{2}-1}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{a_{n}^{2}(q_{n}+1)^{2}-b_{n}^{2}(q_{n}-1)^{2}}{z^{2}(q_{n}^{2}-1)}-\frac{c_{n}(q_{n}^{2}-1)}{2z}+\tfrac{1}{4}q_{n}(q_{n}^{2}-1),

(3.22)

with
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
given by (
3.14
).

We note that

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{2}(a,b,c)=(a-1,b+1,c+2),

and so

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{2n}(a,b,c)=(a-n,b+n,c+2n).

Therefore since

\displaystyle(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+\rho+\varphi),\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda-\rho+\varphi),\lambda-\varphi\big),

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+\rho-\varphi+1),\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda-\rho-\varphi+1),\lambda+\varphi+1\big).

then

\displaystyle(a_{2n},b_{2n},c_{2n})

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n+\lambda+\rho+\varphi),\tfrac{1}{2}(2n+\lambda-\rho+\varphi),2n+\lambda-\varphi\big),

\displaystyle(a_{2n+1},b_{2n+1},c_{2n+1})

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n+\lambda+\rho-\varphi+1),\tfrac{1}{2}(2n+\lambda-\rho-\varphi+1),2n+\lambda+\varphi+1\big).

If
w_{0}(z)
satisfies
P
V
(
1.2
) with
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{8}(\lambda+\rho+\varphi)^{2}
,
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{8}(\lambda-\rho+\varphi)^{2}
and
\gamma=\lambda-\varphi
then

x_{n}=q_{2n}=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{2}^{2n}(w_{0})+1}{\mathcal{R}_{2}^{2n}(w_{0})-1},\qquad y_{n}=q_{2n+1}=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{2}^{2n+1}(w_{0})+1}{\mathcal{R}_{2}^{2n+1}(w_{0})-1},

(3.23)

satisfy (
3.21
).

Remark 3.3
.

Since the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}
involves a sequence of two Bäcklund transformations then it might be expected that the resulting transformation is quadratic in
\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
. Generally if one applies the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}}
followed by the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}
then the resulting transformation is quadratic in
\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{w}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
, but in some cases there is a simplification which happens in this case.

3.3
Third discrete equation: ternary discrete
P
I

Consider the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{3}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}
, which has an inverse
\mathcal{R}_{3}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1}
, then

\displaystyle w_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{3}(w_{n})=1-\frac{2zw_{n}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}+a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(a_{n}+b_{n}-z)w_{n}+b_{n}},

(3.24a)

\displaystyle w_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{3}^{-1}(w_{n})=1+\frac{2zw_{n}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}-a_{n}w_{n}^{2}+(a_{n}-b_{n}-z)w_{n}+b_{n}},

(3.24b)

with
{}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
,
where
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
satisfy

\displaystyle(a_{n+1},b_{n+1},c_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{3}(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}-b_{n}+c_{n}+1\big),\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}-b_{n}-c_{n}+1\big),a_{n}+b_{n}\right),

(3.25a)

\displaystyle(a_{n-1},b_{n-1},c_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{3}^{-1}(a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}-1\big),-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a_{n}+b_{n}-c_{n}-1\big),a_{n}-b_{n}\right).

(3.25b)

These have solution

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3}n+\rho+\lambda\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)+\tfrac{1}{3}\sqrt{3}\,\varphi\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

(3.26a)

\displaystyle b_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3}+\sqrt{3}\,\lambda\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)-\varphi\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

(3.26b)

\displaystyle c_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3}n+\rho-2\lambda\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)-\tfrac{2}{3}\sqrt{3}\,\varphi\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

(3.26c)

with
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\varphi
arbitrary constants (the factor of
\sqrt{3}
is for convenience), i.e.

\begin{split}a_{3n}&=n+\lambda+\rho,\\
a_{3n+1}&=n-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{1}{3},\\
a_{3n+2}&=n-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{2}{3},\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}b_{3n}&=-\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},\\
b_{3n+1}&=\tfrac{3}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},\\
b_{3n+2}&=-\tfrac{3}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}c_{3n}&=n-2\lambda+\rho,\\
c_{3n+1}&=n+\lambda-\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{1}{3},\\
c_{3n+2}&=n+\lambda+\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{2}{3}.\end{split}

which have a ternary symmetry in contrast to the binary symmetry for the parameters discussed in §
3.1
and §
3.2
above.

Setting
w_{n}=1+1/x_{n}
and
w_{n\pm 1}=1+1/x_{n\pm 1}
in (
3.24
) gives

\displaystyle x_{n+1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{a_{n}}{2zx_{n}}-\frac{b_{n}}{2z(x_{n}+1)}+\frac{1}{2}=0,

(3.27a)

\displaystyle x_{n-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{a_{n}}{2zx_{n}}+\frac{b_{n}}{2z(x_{n}+1)}+\frac{1}{2}=0.

(3.27b)

Hence by adding we obtain

x_{n}(x_{n+1}+x_{n-1}+1)+\frac{a_{n}}{z}=0,

(3.28)

which is known as the
ternary
d
P
I
equation

[
32
,
78
]
. We remark that this derivation of (
3.28
) was given by two of us (Clarkson and Mitchell, in collaboration with Dzhamay and Hone), see
[
17
]
.

Since equation (
3.28
) has a ternary symmetry, then it can be written as a system of three equations.
Setting
x_{3n}=X_{n}
,
x_{3n+1}=Y_{n}
and
x_{3n+2}=Z_{n}
in (
3.28
) yields the discrete system

\displaystyle X_{n}(Y_{n}+Z_{n-1}+1)+\frac{a_{3n}}{z}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.29a)

\displaystyle Y_{n}(Z_{n}+X_{n}+1)+\frac{a_{3n+1}}{z}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.29b)

\displaystyle Z_{n}(X_{n+1}+Y_{n}+1)+\frac{a_{3n+2}}{z}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.29c)

Further
x_{n}
satisfies the second-order equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{2x_{n}+1}{2x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{a_{n}^{2}(x_{n}+1)^{2}-b_{n}^{2}x_{n}^{2}}{2z^{2}x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}-\frac{c_{n}x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}{z}+\frac{x_{n}(x_{n}+1)(2x_{n}+1)}{2},

(3.30)

with
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
given by (
3.26
).

We note that

\mathcal{R}_{3}^{3}(a,b,c)=(a+1,b,c+1),

and so

\mathcal{R}_{3}^{3n}(a,b,c)=(a+n,b,c+n).

Therefore since

\displaystyle(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})

\displaystyle=\big(\lambda+\rho,-\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},-2\lambda+\rho\big),

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{3}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},\lambda-\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{1}{3}\big),

\displaystyle(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2})

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{2}{3},-\tfrac{3}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},\lambda+\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{2}{3}\big),

then

\displaystyle(a_{3n},b_{3n},c_{3n})

\displaystyle=\big(n+\lambda+\rho,-\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},n-2\lambda+\rho\big),

\displaystyle(a_{3n+1},b_{3n+1},c_{3n+1})

\displaystyle=\big(n-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{3}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},n+\lambda-\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{1}{3}\big),

\displaystyle(a_{3n+2},b_{3n+2},c_{3n+2})

\displaystyle=\big(n-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{2}{3},-\tfrac{3}{2}\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi+\tfrac{1}{3},n+\lambda+\varphi+\rho+\tfrac{2}{3}\big).

If
w_{0}
satisfies
P
V
(
1.2
) with
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}(\lambda+\rho)^{2}
,
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\varphi-\tfrac{1}{3})^{2}
and
\gamma=-2\lambda+\rho
then

X_{n}=x_{3n}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}_{3}^{3n}(w_{0})-1},\qquad Y_{n}=x_{3n+1}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}_{3}^{3n+1}(w_{0})-1},\qquad Z_{n}=x_{3n+2}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}_{3}^{3n+2}(w_{0})-1},

satisfy (
3.29
).

Remarks 3.4
.

1.

In terms of
w
, the transformation (
3.24a
) is the same as
\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}(w)
.
However, the parameters
(a,b,c)
map differently since

\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}(a,b,c)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(a-b+c-1),\tfrac{1}{2}(-a+b+c-1),a+b\big).

It is straightforward to show that

\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1}(w)=w
, whereas
\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}(w)\not=w
,
so
\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}
is
not
the inverse of
\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1}
.

2.

It is interesting to note that given a solution
w_{0}(z)
of
P
V
(
1.2
) with
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{0}^{2},c_{0}\big)
, the transformation
\mathcal{R}_{1}
(
3.3a
) maps it to the solution
w_{1}(z)
with
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}a_{1}^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{1}^{2},c_{1}\big)
and the transformation
\mathcal{R}_{3}
(
3.24a
) maps it to the solution
w_{3}(z)
with
(\alpha_{3},\beta_{3},\gamma_{3})=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}a_{3}^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{3}^{2},c_{3}\big)
where

w_{1}(z)=w_{3}(z),\qquad a_{1}=a_{3},\qquad b_{1}=-b_{3},\qquad c_{1}=c_{3},

i.e. both transformations map
w_{0}(z)
to the
same
solution of
P
V
. However applying the transformation a second time gives different solutions since

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2}(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})

\displaystyle=(a_{0}+1,b_{0}-1,c_{0}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{3}^{2}(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{0}+b_{0}+c_{0}+1),-\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{0}+b_{0}-c_{0}-1),a_{0}-b_{0}+1\big),

so the transformations
\mathcal{R}_{1}
(
3.3
) and
\mathcal{R}_{3}
(
3.24
) will generate
different
hierarchies of solutions.

3.

The symmetric case of equation (
3.28
), i.e. when
\lambda=\varphi=0
,

x_{n}(x_{n+1}+x_{n-1}+1)+\frac{\rho+\tfrac{1}{3}n}{z}=0,

(3.31)

is a discrete
P
I

[
70
, equation (3.6)]
, whilst the full equation, which was derived from a discrete dressing transformation
[
32
]
, is a discrete form of
P
IV
(
1.11
).

4.

The ternary d
P
I
(
3.28
) is associated with
P
V
(
1.2
), though the asymmetric generalisation of d
P
I
(
1.5
), i.e.

x_{n+1}+x_{n}+x_{n-1}=1+\frac{\lambda n+\rho+(-1)^{n}\varphi}{x_{n}},

(3.32)

with
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\varphi
constants, is associated with
P
IV
(
1.11
) cf.
[
24
,
50
]
, and can be derived from Bäcklund transformations of
P
IV

[
18
,
23
]
. We remark that the continuum limit of (
3.32
) with
\varphi\not=0
is
P
II
(
1.8
) rather than
P
I
(
1.6
), as shown in
[
19
]
.

5.

Recently Clarkson
et al.

[
17
]
studied the special case of the ternary d
P
I
equation given by

v_{n}(v_{n+1}+v_{n-1}+1)=(n+1)\varepsilon,\qquad\varepsilon>0,

(3.33)

with
v_{-1}=0
, which arises in quantum minimal surfaces
[
5
,
40
]
and found a unique solution. The expressions for
v_{3n}
,
v_{3n+1}
and
v_{3n+2}
were written explicitly as ratios of Wronskian determinants in terms of the modified Bessel functions
K_{1/6}(z)
and
K_{5/6}(z)
and were all different showing the three-fold structure of the solutions.

6.

Felder and Hoppe
[
21
]
show that ternary d
P
I
equation (
3.33
) also arises in connection with orthogonal polynomials in the complex plane with respect to the weight

w(z;t)=\exp\left\{-t|z|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathrm{i}(z^{3}+\overline{z}^{3})\right\},\qquad t>0,

with
z=x+\mathrm{i}y\in\mathbb{C}
, and has the
same
unique solution as in
[
17
]
.
Teodorescu
et al.

[
77
]
, see also
[
76
, equation (26)]
, show that equation (
3.33
) arises in the theory of random normal matrices
[
83
]
, which motivated the study in
[
21
]
.

7.

A study of the asymptotics of solutions of (
3.33
) is given by Aptekarev and Novokshenov
[
2
]
.

3.4
Fourth discrete equation: new equation with ternary symmetry

It is possible to derive a discrete equation relating the three solutions
x_{n}
,
x_{n+2}
and
x_{n-2}
of the ternary d
P
I
equation (
3.28
) as illustrated in the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that
x_{n}
,
x_{n+2}
and
x_{n-2}
are solutions of the ternary
d
P
I
equation (
3.28
),
then they satisfy the discrete equation

\frac{a_{n+1}}{x_{n+2}+x_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{n-1}}{x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1}=z+\frac{a_{n}}{x_{n}},

(3.34)

where
a_{n}
is given by (
3.26a
), i.e.

a_{n}=\tfrac{1}{3}n+\rho+\lambda\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)+\tfrac{1}{3}\sqrt{3}\,\varphi\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

(3.35)

with
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\varphi
arbitrary constants.

Proof.

Letting
n\to n-1
in equation (
3.28
) gives

x_{n-1}(x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1)+\frac{a_{n-1}}{z}=0.

Solving this equation and (
3.28
) for
x_{n+1}
and
x_{n-1}
gives

x_{n+1}=\frac{a_{n-1}}{z(x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1)}-1-\frac{a_{n}}{zx_{n}},\qquad x_{n-1}=-\frac{a_{n-1}}{z(x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1)}.

Letting
n\to n+2
in the expression for
x_{n-1}
gives

x_{n+1}=-\frac{a_{n+1}}{z(x_{n+2}+x_{n}+1)}.

Equating the two expressions for
x_{n+1}
gives the result.
∎

An alternative proof is to consider the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{4}=\mathcal{R}_{3}^{2}
, with
\mathcal{R}_{4}^{-1}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,1}^{2}
, then

\displaystyle w_{n+2}=\mathcal{R}_{4}(w_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}}\left\{1+\frac{(a_{n}-b_{n}+c_{n}+1)(w_{n}-1)^{2}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}+a_{n}w_{n}^{2}-(2a_{n}+c_{n}+1-z)w_{n}+a_{n}+c_{n}+1}\right\},

(3.36a)

\displaystyle w_{n-2}=\mathcal{R}_{4}^{-1}(w_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}}\left\{1+\frac{(a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}-1)(w_{n}-1)^{2}}{\displaystyle zw_{n}^{\prime}-a_{n}w_{n}^{2}+(2a_{n}+c_{n}-1-z)w_{n}-a_{n}-c_{n}+1}\right\},

(3.36b)

with
{}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}
and
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
are given by (
3.26
). Letting
w_{n}=1+1/x_{n}
and
w_{n\pm 2}=1+1/x_{n\pm 2}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{a_{n}-b_{n}+c_{n}+1}{x_{n+2}+x_{n}+1}

\displaystyle=z-\frac{z}{x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{(a_{n}-b_{n})x_{n}+a_{n}}{x_{n}(x_{n}+1)},

(3.37a)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}-1}{x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1}

\displaystyle=z+\frac{z}{x_{n}(x_{n}+1)}\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}+\frac{(a_{n}+b_{n})x_{n}+a_{n}}{x_{n}(x_{n}+1)},

(3.37b)

and so by adding we obtain

\frac{a_{n+1}}{x_{n+2}+x_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{n-1}}{x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1}=z+\frac{a_{n}}{x_{n}},

since from (
3.25
),
a_{n+1}=\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{n}-b_{n}+c_{n}+1)
and
a_{n-1}=\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}-1)
.
The parameters
\widetilde{a}_{n}
,
\widetilde{b}_{n}
and
\widetilde{c}_{n}
satisfy

\begin{split}\widetilde{a}_{n+1}&=\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{a}_{n}+\widetilde{b}_{n}+\widetilde{c}_{n}+1),\\
\widetilde{b}_{n+1}&=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{a}_{n}+\widetilde{b}_{n}-\widetilde{c}_{n}-1),\\
\widetilde{c}_{n+1}&=\widetilde{a}_{n}-\widetilde{b}_{n}+1,\end{split}\qquad\quad\begin{split}\widetilde{a}_{n-1}&=\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{a}_{n}-\widetilde{b}_{n}+\widetilde{c}_{n}-1),\\
\widetilde{b}_{n-1}&=\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{a}_{n}-\widetilde{b}_{n}-\widetilde{c}_{n}+1),\\
\widetilde{c}_{n-1}&=\widetilde{a}_{n}+\widetilde{b}_{n}-1,\end{split}

which have solution

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{3}n+\rho+\lambda\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)+\tfrac{1}{3}\sqrt{3}\,\varphi\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3}-\sqrt{3}\,\lambda\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)+\varphi\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{3}n+\rho-2\lambda\cos\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big)-\tfrac{2}{3}\sqrt{3}\,\varphi\sin\big(\tfrac{2}{3}\pi n\big),

with
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\varphi
arbitrary constants.
We remark that the Bäcklund transformation (
3.36
) has similar structure to the Bäcklund transformation
\mathcal{R}_{2}
(
3.13
), except the parameters have a ternary symmetry rather than a binary symmetry. We note that since
\mathcal{R}_{3}=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{4}=\mathcal{R}_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}

as
\mathcal{T}_{1,-1,1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{-1,1,-1}=\mathcal{I}
, the identity.

Corollary 3.6
.

If
x_{n}
satisfies ternary
d
P
I
equation (
3.28
) and
a_{n}
is given by (
3.35
), then
q_{n}=x_{2n}
and
p_{n}=x_{2n+1}
satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2n+1}}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{2n-1}}{q_{n}+q_{n-1}+1}

\displaystyle=z+\frac{a_{2n}}{q_{n}},

(3.38a)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2n+2}}{p_{n+1}+p_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{2n}}{p_{n}+p_{n-1}+1}

\displaystyle=z+\frac{a_{2n+1}}{p_{n}},

(3.38b)

respectively. Further
X_{n}=x_{3n}
,
Y_{n}=x_{3n+1}
and
Z_{n}=x_{3n+2}
satisfy
the system

\displaystyle\frac{a_{3n+1}}{X_{n}+Z_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{3n-1}}{X_{n}+Y_{n-1}+1}

\displaystyle=z+\frac{a_{3n}}{X_{n}},

(3.39a)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{3n+2}}{X_{n+1}+Y_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{3n}}{Y_{n}+Z_{n-1}+1}

\displaystyle=z+\frac{a_{3n+1}}{Y_{n}},

(3.39b)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{3n+3}}{Y_{n+1}+Z_{n}+1}+\frac{a_{3n+1}}{X_{n}+Z_{n}+1}

\displaystyle=z+\frac{a_{3n+2}}{Z_{n}}.

(3.39c)

Proof.

Letting
n\to 2n
and
n\to 2n+1
in (
3.34
) and then setting
q_{n}=x_{2n}
and
p_{n}=x_{2n+1}
, respectively, gives (
3.38
). Similarly for the system (
3.39
), which is equivalent to the system (
3.29
).
∎

3.5
Differential-difference equations

In the previous subsections, we have obtained discrete equations by eliminating the derivative between two equations, i.e. by adding the equations in (
3.5
), (
3.15
) and (
3.27
).
If instead we subtract the equations then we obtain differential-difference equations.

(i)

Subtracting the equations in (
3.6
) gives the
modified Volterra lattice

[
82
]

\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}=\tfrac{1}{4}(q_{n}^{2}-1)(q_{n+1}-q_{n-1}).

which is a special case of equation (
3.40
) below.

(ii)

Subtracting the equations in (
3.17
) gives

z\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1-q_{n}^{2})\left(\frac{2n+2\lambda+1}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}}-\frac{2n+2\lambda-1}{q_{n}+q_{n-1}}\right).

which is a non-autonomous special case of equation (
3.41
) below.

(iii)

Subtracting the equations in (
3.27
) gives

z\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}=zx_{n}(x_{n}+1)(x_{n+1}-x_{n-1})-{b_{n}x_{n}},

with
b_{n}
given by (
3.26b
).

(iv)

Subtracting the equations in (
3.37
) gives

z\frac{\mathrm{d}{x_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}=-x_{n}(x_{n}+1)\left(\frac{a_{n+1}}{x_{n+2}+x_{n}+1}-\frac{a_{n-1}}{x_{n}+x_{n-2}+1}\right)-b_{n}x_{n},

with
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
given by (
3.26
). Consequently
q_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{2n}+1)
and
p_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{2n+1}+1)
satisfy

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{q_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=(1-q_{n}^{2})\left(\frac{a_{2n+1}}{q_{n+1}+q_{n}}-\frac{a_{2n-1}}{q_{n}+q_{n-1}}\right)-b_{2n}(q_{n}-1),

\displaystyle z\frac{\mathrm{d}{p_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=(1-p_{n}^{2})\left(\frac{a_{2n+2}}{p_{n+1}+p_{n}}-\frac{a_{2n}}{p_{n}+p_{n-1}}\right)-b_{2n+1}(p_{n}-1),

which are non-autonomous special cases of equation (
3.41
) below.

Remark 3.7
.

Yamilov
[
85
]
gives a classification of (autonomous) Volterra-type equations. The first two are:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{u_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=P(u_{n})(u_{n+1}-u_{n-1}),

(3.40)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{u_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}

\displaystyle=P(u_{n}^{2})\left(\frac{1}{u_{n+1}+u_{n}}-\frac{1}{u_{n}+u_{n-1}}\right),

(3.41)

where
P(u)=au^{2}+bu+c
, with
a
,
b
and
c
constants.

4
Rational solutions of
P
V

In this section we describe the rational solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
),
which are classified in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

Equation (
1.2
) has a rational solution if and only if one of the following holds:

(i)

\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}
,
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+2n+1+\kappa)^{2}
,
\gamma=\kappa
, for
m\geq 1
;

(ii)

\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\kappa)^{2}
,
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\varepsilon\kappa)^{2}
,
\gamma=m+\varepsilon n
, with
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, provided that
m\not=0
or
n\not=0
;

(iii)

\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}
,
\beta=-\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}
,
\gamma=\kappa
, provided that
m\not=0
or
n\not=0
,

where
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\kappa
is an arbitrary constant,
together with the solutions obtained through the symmetries

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}:\qquad

\displaystyle{w_{1}}({z};{\alpha_{1}},{\beta_{1}},{\gamma_{1}},-\tfrac{1}{2})={w(-z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,-\tfrac{1}{2})},

\displaystyle({\alpha_{1}},{\beta_{1}},{\gamma_{1}},-\tfrac{1}{2})=(\alpha,\beta,-\gamma,-\tfrac{1}{2}),

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}:\qquad

\displaystyle{w_{2}}({z};{\alpha_{2}},{\beta_{2}},{\gamma_{3}},-\tfrac{1}{2})=\frac{1}{w(z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,-\tfrac{1}{2})},

\displaystyle({\alpha_{2}},{\beta_{2}},{\gamma_{3}},-\tfrac{1}{2})=(-\beta,-\alpha,-\gamma,-\tfrac{1}{2}),

(4.2)

where
w(z;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,-\tfrac{1}{2})
is a solution of
P
V
(
1.2
).

Proof.

See Kitaev, Law and McLeod
[
46
, Theorem 1.1]
; also
[
38
, Theorem 40.3]
.∎

Remarks 4.2
.

1.

Kitaev, Law and McLeod
[
46
, Theorem 1.1]
give four cases, though their cases (I) and (II) are related by the symmetry (
4.2
).
Further Kitaev, Law and McLeod
[
46
, Theorem 1.2]
prove that if
\gamma\not\in\mathbb{Z}
in case (i), then the rational solution is unique and if
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}
then there are at most two rational solutions, as illustrated in
[
16
]
; see also
[
3
,
4
]
.

2.

Rational solutions in case (i) are expressed in terms of
generalised Laguerre polynomials
, which are discussed in §
5.1
, and in cases (ii) and (iii) in terms of
generalised Umemura polynomials
, discussed in §
5.2
.

4.1
Generalised Laguerre polynomials

Rational solutions in case (i) of Theorem
4.1
are expressed in terms of
generalised Laguerre polynomials
, which are written in terms of a determinant of Laguerre polynomials, or equivalently as a Wronskian, see
[
16
, §3]
. These rational solutions are special cases of the solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
) expressible in terms of the Kummer functions
M(a,b,z)
and
U(a,b,z)
discussed in
[
51
,
65
]
since

U(-n,\alpha+1,z)=(-1)^{n}(\alpha+1)_{n}M(-n,\alpha+1,z)=(-1)^{n}n!\,L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(z),

(4.3)

with
L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(z)
the Laguerre polynomial

L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(z)=\frac{z^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{e}^{z}}{n!}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{n}}\left(z^{n+\alpha}\,\mathrm{e}^{-z}\right),\qquad n\geq 0,

(4.4)

see
[
62
, equation (13.6.19)]
.
First we define the generalised Laguerre polynomial
T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
; properties of it are discussed in
[
6
,
16
]
.

Definition 4.3
.

The
generalised Laguerre polynomial

T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
, which is a polynomial of degree
(m+1)n
, is defined by

T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\det\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{j+k}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{j+k}}L_{m+n}^{(\mu+1)}(z)\right]_{j,k=0}^{n-1},\qquad m\geq 0,\quad n\geq 1,

(4.5)

where
L_{n}^{(\mu)}(z)
is the Laguerre polynomial (
4.4
).

For case (i) in Theorem
4.1
, Kitaev, Law and McLeod
[
46
]
state that rational solutions arise when

\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell^{2},\qquad\beta=-\tfrac{1}{2}(k+\varepsilon\gamma)^{2},\qquad\varepsilon=\pm 1,

where
\ell>0
and
k+\ell
is odd, with
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\beta\not=0
when
|k|<\ell
. Since the sign of
\gamma
can be changed by letting
z\to-z
, we set
\varepsilon=1
, without loss of generality. There are three cases (a),
k>\ell
, (b),
|k|\leq\ell
and (c),
-k>\ell
, which are given in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.4
.

Suppose that
T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
is the generalised Laguerre polynomial given by (
4.5
) and
L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(z)
is the Laguerre polynomial, then
P
V
(
1.2
) has the following three sets of rational solutions.

(a)

If
\ell=m
and
k=m+2n+1
, with
m\geq 1
, so
k+\ell=2m+2n+1
, then for
n\geq 1

{w}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu)}(z)\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}(z)}{T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+2n+1+\mu)^{2},\mu\big).

(4.6a)

In the case when
n=0
, then for
m\geq 1

{w}_{m,0}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{L_{m}^{(\mu+1)}(z)}{L_{m-1}^{(\mu+1)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+1+\mu)^{2},\mu\big).

(4.6b)

(b)

If
\ell=m+n+1
and
k=m-n
, with
m\geq 0
, so
k+\ell=2m+1
, then for
m\geq 1

{v}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{m-n+\mu}{m+n+1}\,\frac{T_{m,n}^{(\mu-2n)}(z)\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-2n-2)}(z)}{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu-2n)}(z)\,T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-2n-2)}(z)},\quad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+n+1)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m-n+\mu)^{2},\mu\big).

(4.7a)

In the case when
m=0
and
n\geq 1
then

\displaystyle{v}_{0,n}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle={\frac{n-\mu}{n+1}\,\frac{L_{n}^{(-\mu-1)}(-z)}{L_{n+1}^{(-\mu-1)}(-z)}},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(n+1)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\mu)^{2},\mu\big),

(4.7b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}_{0,n}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{L_{n+1}^{(-1-\mu)}(-z)}{L_{n}^{(-1-\mu)}(-z)},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(n+1)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\mu)^{2},\mu+2\big).

(4.7c)

In the case when
n=0
and
m\geq 0
then

\displaystyle{v}_{m,0}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{m+\mu}{m+1}\,\frac{L^{(\mu-1)}_{m}(z)}{L^{(\mu-1)}_{m+1}(z)},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+1)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\mu)^{2},\mu\big),

(4.7d)

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}_{m,0}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{L^{(\mu-1)}_{m+1}(z)}{L^{(\mu-1)}_{m}(z)},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+1)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\mu)^{2},\mu-2\big).

(4.7e)

(c)

If
\ell=m
and
k=-m-2n-1
, with
m\geq 1
and
n\geq 0
, so
k+\ell=-2m-1
, then
{u}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)={w}_{m,n}^{(-\mu)}(-z)
, i.e.

{u}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu-2m-2n)}(z)\,T_{n,m}^{(\mu-2m-2n-2)}(z)}{T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu-2m-2n)}(z)\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-2m-2n-2)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+2n+1-\mu)^{2},\mu\big).

(4.8a)

In the case when
n=0
, then for
m\geq 1

{u}_{m,0}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{L_{m}^{(1-\mu)}(-z)}{L_{m-1}^{(1-\mu)}(-z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(m+1-\mu)^{2},\mu\big).

(4.8b)

Proof.

See Clarkson and Dunning
[
16
]
. The solutions when either
m=0
or
n=0
follow from recurrence relations for the Laguerre polynomial, cf.
[
62
, §18.9]
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

The rational solutions in Theorem
4.4
can also be written as logarithmic derivatives

\displaystyle w_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{m}\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}(z)}{T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)}-\frac{z-m-2n-1-\mu}{m},

(4.9a)

\displaystyle v_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{m+n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu-2n)}(z)}{T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-2n-2)}(z)}+1,

(4.9b)

\displaystyle u_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{m}\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu-2m-2n)}}{T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-2m-2n-2)}}+\frac{z+m+2n+1-\mu}{m}.

(4.9c)

Recall that solutions of the discrete equations (
3.7
) and (
3.18
) also satisfy the second-order equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{q}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{q}{q^{2}-1}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{q}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{q}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{2\alpha(q+1)^{2}+2\beta(q-1)^{2}}{z^{2}(q^{2}-1)}-\frac{\gamma(q^{2}-1)}{2z}+\tfrac{1}{4}q(q^{2}-1),

(4.10)

and solutions of the ternary d
P
I
(
3.28
) also satisfy the second-order equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{x}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}^{2}}=\frac{2x+1}{2x(x+1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{x}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\right)^{\!2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{\mathrm{d}{x}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}-\frac{\alpha(x+1)^{2}+\beta x^{2}}{z^{2}x(x+1)}-\frac{\gamma x(x+1)}{z}+\frac{x(x+1)(2x+1)}{2}.

(4.11)

In the following Lemma we derive the solutions of equations (
4.10
) and (
4.11
) in terms of generalised Laguerre polynomials.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
w_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
,
v_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
and
u_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
are rational solutions of
P
V
defined in Theorem
4.4
and
T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
is the generalised Laguerre polynomial given by (
4.5
), then

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{w_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)+1}{w_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=1+2\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}(z)}{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}(z)},

(4.12a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{v_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)+1}{v_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=-1+\frac{2\mu}{z}+2\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-2n-1)}(z)}{T_{m,n}^{(\mu-2n-1)}(z)},

(4.12b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{u_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)+1}{u_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=1+2\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{n,m}^{(\mu-2m-2n-1)}}{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu-2m-2n-1)}},

(4.12c)

are solutions of equation (
4.10
) and

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{1}{w_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}(z)}{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}(z)},

(4.13a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{1}{v_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=-1+\frac{\mu}{z}+\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-2n-1)}(z)}{T_{m,n}^{(\mu-2n-1)}(z)},

(4.13b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)=\frac{1}{u_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{n,m}^{(\mu-2m-2n-1)}}{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu-2m-2n-1)}},

(4.13c)

are solutions of equation (
4.11
).

Proof.

Since

\frac{w+1}{w-1}=1+\frac{2}{w-1},

(4.14)

then we prove (
4.13a
) first.
By definition

{w}_{m,n}^{(\mu)}=\frac{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}}{T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}},

and so

\frac{1}{w_{m,n}^{(\mu)}-1}=\frac{T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}}{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}-T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}}.

Also

\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}}{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}=\frac{{\rm D}_{z}\big(T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}\big)}{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}=\frac{T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}}{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}-T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)}},

since from the identity (
A.1c
)

{\rm D}_{z}\big(T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}\big)=T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)},

where
{\rm D}_{z}(f{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}g)
is the Hirota operator defined by

{{\rm D}_{z}(f{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}g)=\frac{\mathrm{d}{f}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}g-f\frac{\mathrm{d}{g}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}},

(4.15)

and from the identity (
A.2c
)

T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}=\ T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}-T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-2,n+1}^{(\mu)},

which completes the proof of (
4.13a
).
The identity (
4.12a
) follows from equation (
4.14
).

By definition

{v}_{m,n}^{(\mu+2n+1)}=\frac{m+n+\mu+1}{m+n+1}\,\frac{T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}{T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}},

and so

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{v}_{m,n}^{(\mu+2n+1)}-1}+1

\displaystyle=\frac{(m+n+\mu+1)T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}{(m+n+\mu+1)T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}-(m+n+1)T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}.

From (
4.7a
) and (
4.9b
) it follows

z{\rm D}_{z}\big(T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}\big)=(m+n+\mu+1)T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}-(m+n+1)T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}\,T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)},

and since from (
A.1b
)

{\rm D}_{z}\big(T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}\big)=T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)},

then we have

zT_{m,n}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}=(m+n+\mu+1)T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}-(m+n+1)T_{m,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}\,T_{m-1,n}^{(\mu+1)}.

Therefore

\frac{1}{{v}_{m,n}^{(\mu+2n+1)}-1}+1=\frac{m+n+\mu+1}{z}\,\frac{T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}},

and

\frac{m+n+\mu+1}{z}+\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}}{T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}}=\frac{m+n+\mu+1}{z}\,\frac{T_{m,n}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu-1)}}{T_{m-1,n+1}^{(\mu)}\,T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}}.

Hence we obtain (
4.13b
). The identity (
4.12b
) follows from equation (
4.14
).

By definition

u_{m,n}^{(\mu+2m+2n+1)}(z)=\frac{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n,m}^{(\mu-1)}}{T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)}},

so

\frac{1}{u_{m,n}^{(\mu+2m+2n+1)}(z)-1}=\frac{T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)}}{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n,m}^{(\mu-1)}-T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)}}.

Also

\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{T_{n,m}^{(\mu)}}{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu)}}=\frac{{\rm D}_{z}\big(T_{n,m}^{(\mu)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu)}\big)}{T_{n,m}^{(\mu)}\,T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu)}}=\frac{T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)}}{T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n,m}^{(\mu-1)}-T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)}},

since from the identity (
A.1f
)

{\rm D}_{z}\big(T_{n,m}^{(\mu)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu)}\big)=T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)},

and from the identity (
A.2b
)

T_{n,m}^{(\mu)}\,T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu)}=T_{n-1,m}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n,m}^{(\mu-1)}-T_{n,m-1}^{(\mu+1)}\,T_{n-1,m+1}^{(\mu-1)},

which completes the proof of (
4.13c
).
The identity (
4.12c
) follows from equation (
4.14
).
∎

4.2
Generalised Umemura polynomials

Rational solutions in cases (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
4.1
are expressed in terms of
generalised Umemura polynomials
.
Umemura
[
79
]
defined some polynomials through a differential-difference equation to describe rational solutions of
P
V
(
1.2
); see also
[
13
,
58
,
84
]
.
Subsequently these were generalised by Masuda, Ohta and Kajiwara
[
53
]
, who defined the generalised Umemura polynomial
R_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
through coupled differential-difference equations and gave a representation as the determinant

R_{m,n}^{(\ell)}(z)=\left|\begin{matrix}q_{1}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&q_{-m+2}^{(\ell)}&q_{-m+1}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&q_{-m-n+2}^{(\ell)}\\[2.0pt]
q_{3}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&q_{-m+4}^{(\ell)}&q_{-m+3}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&q_{-m-n+4}^{(\ell)}\\[2.0pt]
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\[2.0pt]
q_{2m-1}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&q_{m}^{(\ell)}&q_{m-1}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&q_{m-n}^{(\ell)}\\[2.0pt]
p_{n-m}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&p_{n+1}^{(\ell)}&p_{n}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&p_{2n-1}^{(\ell)}\\[2.0pt]
p_{n-m-2}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&p_{n-1}^{(\ell)}&p_{n-2}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&p_{2n-3}^{(\ell)}\\[2.0pt]
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\[2.0pt]
p_{-n-m+2}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&p_{-n+1}^{(\ell)}&p_{-n+2}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&p_{1}^{(\ell)}\end{matrix}\right|,

(4.16)

where
p_{k}^{(\ell)}(z)=L_{k}^{(\ell-1)}(\tfrac{1}{2}z)
and
q_{k}^{(\ell)}(z)=L_{k}^{(\ell-1)}(-\tfrac{1}{2}z)
, with
p_{k}^{(\ell)}=q_{k}^{(\ell)}=0
for
k<0
, and
L_{k}^{(\alpha)}(z)
is the Laguerre polynomial (
4.4
). Recently Masuda
[
52
]
studied properties of zeros of the polynomial
R_{m,n}^{(\ell)}(z)
.

Rather than use (
4.16
) for the generalised Umemura polynomial, we prefer to use an equivalent representation given in Definition
4.7
, which we find simpler to compute and useful to prove algebraic and combinatorial properties of the polynomial, though we do not pursue this further here.

Definition 4.7
.

The
generalised Umemura polynomial

U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
is given by the Wronskian

U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=c_{m,n}\exp(\tfrac{1}{2}mz)\Wr\!\left({\varphi^{(\kappa)}_{1}},{\varphi^{(\kappa)}_{3}},\ldots,{\varphi^{(\kappa)}_{2m-1}};{\psi^{(\kappa)}_{1}},{\psi^{(\kappa)}_{3}},\ldots,{\psi^{(\kappa)}_{2n-1}}\right),\quad m,n\geq 0,

(4.17)

where

c_{m,n}=(-1)^{m}\,2^{(m+n)(m+n+1)/2}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{(2j+1)!}{j!}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{(2k+1)!}{k!},

(4.18)

{\varphi^{(\kappa)}_{n}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{-z/2}\,L_{n}^{(\kappa)}(\tfrac{1}{2}z)}
and
{\psi^{(\kappa)}_{n}(z)=L_{n}^{(\kappa)}(-\tfrac{1}{2}z)}
,
with
\kappa
a parameter, and
L_{n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
is the Laguerre polynomial (
4.4
) and
U_{0,0}^{(\kappa)}(z)=1
.

Remarks 4.8
.

1.

The generalised Laguerre polynomial
T_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
is a Wronskian involving one sequence of Laguerre polynomials, whereas the generalised Umemura polynomial
U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
involves two sequences of Laguerre polynomials, unless either
m=0
or
n=0
. This is analogous to the situation for rational solutions of
P
IV
(
1.11
), where one family of rational solutions involves one sequence of Hermite polynomials and a second family involves two sequences of Hermite polynomials, cf.
[
14
,
60
]
.

2.

The generalised Umemura polynomial
U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
given by (
4.17
) is a monic polynomial of degree
\tfrac{1}{2}m(m+1)+\tfrac{1}{2}n(n+1)
and has the symmetry

U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=U_{n,m}^{(-\kappa-2m-2n)}(z).

(4.19)

3.

The polynomials
R_{m,n}^{(\ell)}(z)
and
U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
given by (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) respectively, are equivalent, which can be shown using matrix row and column operations. Further the two polynomials are related as follows

U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=c_{m,n}(-1)^{m+n(n+1)/2}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{(m+n)(m+n-1)/2}R_{m,n}^{(-\kappa-m-n)}(z),

with
c_{m,n}
given by (
4.18
).

4.

When
m=0
, the polynomial
U_{0,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
is equivalent to the polynomial defined by Umemura
[
79
]
through a differential-difference equation. Also
U_{0,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
is equivalent to the determinant
\tau_{m}^{(n)}(z)
defined in Theorem
5.1
below; see also
[
58
]
. Due to the symmetry (
4.19
), the same is true for the polynomial
U_{m,0}^{(\kappa)}(z)
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Suppose that
U_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
is the generalised Umemura polynomial given by (
4.17
).

(a)

In case (ii) of Theorem
4.1
,
P
V
(
1.2
) has the rational solutions for
m\geq 1
and
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\kappa)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\kappa)^{2},m+n\big),

(4.20a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa-2n+3)}(z)\,U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2n-1)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa-2n+1)}(z)\,U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2n+1)}(z)},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\kappa)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\kappa)^{2},m-n\big),

(4.20b)

In the case when
n=0
, then for
m\geq 1

\widehat{w}_{m,0}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\widehat{v}_{m,0}^{(\kappa)}(z)=-\frac{U_{m,0}^{(2\kappa-1)}(z)\,U_{m-1,0}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)}{U_{m,0}^{(2\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{m-1,0}^{(2\kappa)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\kappa)^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa^{2},m\big),

(4.20c)

and in the case when
m=0
then for
n\geq 1

\widehat{w}_{0,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\widehat{v}_{0,n}^{(-\kappa)}(z)=-\frac{U_{0,n}^{(2\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{0,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{0,n}^{(2\kappa-1)}(z)\,U_{0,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\kappa)^{2},n\big).

(4.20d)

(b)

In case (iii) of Theorem
4.1
,
P
V
(
1.2
) has the rational solution for
m\geq 1
and
n\geq 1

\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=-\,\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{m,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2},m+n+\kappa\big).

(4.21a)

In the case when

n=0
, then for
m\geq 1

\widehat{u}_{m,0}^{(\kappa)}(z)=-\,\frac{U_{m,0}^{(\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m,1}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)}{U_{m-1,0}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{m+1,0}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(m+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2},-\tfrac{1}{8},m+\kappa\big),

(4.21b)

and in the case when
m=0
then for
n\geq 1

\widehat{u}_{0,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=-\,\frac{U_{0,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{0,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)}{U_{0,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)\,U_{1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)},\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\big(\tfrac{1}{8},-\tfrac{1}{2}(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2},n+\kappa\big).

(4.21c)

Proof.

See Masuda, Ohta and Kajiwara
[
53
, Theorem 1.1]
.∎

Remark 4.10
.

The rational solutions in Theorem
4.9
can also be written as logarithmic derivatives

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{m+\kappa}\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)}-\frac{n+\kappa}{m+\kappa},

(4.22a)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa+n)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{m+n+\kappa}\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+1)}(z)}{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa+1)}(z)}-\frac{\kappa}{m+n+\kappa},

(4.22b)

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{2z}{2m+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)}{U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)}+1.

(4.22c)

In the following Lemma we derive the solutions of equations (
4.10
) and (
4.11
) in terms of generalised Umemura polynomials.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
,
\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
and
\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)
are rational solutions of
P
V
defined in Theorem
4.9
and
U_{m,n}^{(\mu)}(z)
is the generalised Umemura polynomial given by (
4.17
), then

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\frac{\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)+1}{\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)},

(4.23a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\frac{\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)+1}{\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa-2n+1)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa-2n+1)}(z)},

(4.23b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{U}}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\frac{\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)+1}{\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(m+n+\kappa)}{z}+2\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)}{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)},

(4.23c)

are solutions of (
4.10
)
and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\frac{1}{\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)},

(4.24a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\frac{1}{\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa-2n+1)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa-2n+1)}(z)},

(4.24b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=\frac{1}{\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(m+n+\kappa)}{z}+\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)}{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)}

(4.24c)

are solutions of (
4.11
).

Proof.

We note that (
4.24
) follow from (
4.23
) using (
4.14
).
By definition

\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=-\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)},

so that we have

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)+1}{\widehat{w}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)-1}=\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)-U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)+U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)}=\frac{{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z){\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\big)}{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)},

where
{\rm D}_{z}(f{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}g)
is the Hirota operator (
4.15
).
From the identities (
A.9b
) and (
A.10b
)

\displaystyle 4{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z){\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\big)

\displaystyle=U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)-U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z),

\displaystyle 2U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)

\displaystyle=U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)+U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z),

respectively, therefore

\frac{2{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z){\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\big)}{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)}=\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)-U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)+U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)},

which proves (
4.23a
).

By definition

\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa+n-1/2)}(z)=-\frac{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}(z)\,U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}(z)\,U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}(z)},

so that we have

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa+n-1/2)}(z)+1}{\widehat{v}_{m,n}^{(\kappa+n-1/2)}(z)-1}=\frac{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}-U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}}{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}+U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}}{U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}}=\frac{{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}\big)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}}.

From the identities (
A.9a
) and (
A.10a
)

\displaystyle 4{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}\big)

\displaystyle=U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}-U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)},

\displaystyle 2U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}

\displaystyle=U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}+U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)},

respectively, therefore

\frac{2{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}\big)}{U_{m,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n}^{(2\kappa)}}=\frac{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}-U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}}{U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa-2)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa+2)}+U_{m,n}^{(2\kappa)}\,U_{m-1,n-1}^{(2\kappa)}},

which proves (
4.23b
).

By definition

\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}(z)=-\,\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{m,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)}{U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}(z)\,U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}(z)},

and so

\frac{\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}+1}{\widehat{u}_{m,n}^{(\kappa)}-1}=\frac{U_{m,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}-U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}}{U_{m,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}+U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}}.

Also

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}{}}{{\mathrm{d}{z}}}\ln\frac{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}}{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}}+\frac{m+n+\kappa}{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}\big)}{U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}}+\frac{m+n+\kappa}{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{z{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}\big)+(m+n+\kappa)U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}}{zU_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}}

It can be shown that

\displaystyle U_{m,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}+U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}

\displaystyle=2zU_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)},

(4.25)

\displaystyle U_{m,n-1}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n+1}^{(\kappa-1)}-U_{m-1,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m+1,n}^{(\kappa-1)}

\displaystyle=4z{\rm D}_{z}\big(U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}{\,\scriptscriptstyle\bullet\,}U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)}\big)+4(m+n+\kappa)U_{m,n}^{(\kappa+1)}\,U_{m,n}^{(\kappa-1)},

(4.26)

which proves (
4.23c
).
Equation (
4.25
) is the identity (
A.13
) and
using computer algebra, we have verified equation (
4.26
) for several small values of
m
and
n
.
∎

5
Rational solutions of the discrete equations

In this section we discuss rational solutions of the discrete equations derived in §
3
. It is known that there are rational solutions of the d
P
II
equation (
1.7
), see
[
42
,
43
,
45
,
58
]
. This is illustrated in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

Define
\tau_{m}^{(n)}(z)
to be the
m\times m
determinant given by

\tau_{m}^{(n)}(z)=\left|\begin{matrix}p^{(n)}_{m}&p^{(n)}_{m+1}&\cdots&p^{(n)}_{2m-1}\\
p^{(n)}_{m-2}&p^{(n)}_{m-1}&\cdots&p^{(n)}_{2m-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
p^{(n)}_{-m+2}&p^{(n)}_{-m+3}&\cdots&p^{(n)}_{1}\end{matrix}\right|,

(5.1)

where
p^{(n)}_{k}(z)=L^{(n)}_{k}(\tfrac{1}{2}z)
, with
L^{(n)}_{k}(z)
the Laguerre polynomial, for
k\geq 0
and
p^{(n)}_{k}(z)=0
for
k<0
,
then

q_{n}(z;m)=\frac{\tau_{m+1}^{(n+1)}(z)\,\tau_{m}^{(n-1)}(z)}{\tau_{m+1}^{(n)}(z)\,\tau_{m}^{(n)}(z)}-1,

(5.2)

satisfies

q_{n+1}+q_{n-1}=\frac{4}{z}\frac{(n+1)q_{n}-m-1}{1-q_{n}^{2}},

(5.3)

which is
d
P
II
(
1.7
) with
\zeta=4/z
,
\lambda=1
and
\rho=-m-1
.

Proof.

See Kajiwara, Yamamoto and Ohta
[
45
]
.∎

Remarks 5.2
.
